Gaussian Approximation and Multiplier Bootstrap for Federated Linear Stochastic Approximation
Ilya Levin ∗, Maksim Shuklin ∗, Eric Moulines, Paul Mangold, Sergey Samsonov

Abstract
In this paper, we establish Berry-Esseen-type bounds for federated linear stochastic approximation (LSA). Our results provide the first federated Gaussian approximations for LSA that explicitly capture communication-computation trade-offs and heterogeneity-aware error terms, quantifying the effects of local step size, number of local updates, and heterogeneity on convergence rates. We present results for both (i) constant step size regime and (ii) decreasing step size with an increasing number of local iterations, recovering the recent rates of 5 as a special case. As a primary application of our results, we develop an online multiplier bootstrap procedure for inference on the last iterate, which avoids explicit estimation of the asymptotic covariance matrix, and obtain non-asymptotic validity guarantees for this procedure.



1
Introduction

Federated learning
[
35
,
22
]
enables collaborative training of machine learning models across distributed data sources. In this context, the Federated Averaging (FedAvg) algorithm
[
35
]
is considered the most fundamental algorithm in federated learning. It reduces communication complexity by using local updates with periodic aggregation
[
51
,
24
,
57
]
. Most of the federated learning literature adopts an optimization perspective, establishing convergence rates and communication complexity for variants of the FedAvg optimization scheme
[
24
,
23
,
27
,
36
,
38
]
. At the same time, an important question concerns inference procedures that aim to provide confidence intervals for parameters of interest or their functionals. The most challenging aspect of theoretical grounding in such algorithms is the Gaussian approximation (GAR) for the underlying model parameters. While there is a growing number of results studying the rates of Gaussian approximation for SGD
[
47
,
48
]
and other stochastic approximation algorithms
[
44
,
58
,
59
,
6
]
, the number of related contributions to the analysis of federated learning algorithms remains limited.

To our knowledge, the few existing works on statistical inference for FedAvg
[
28
,
21
]
establish only
asymptotic
convergence to a limiting Gaussian distribution. A notable exception is the recent work of
5
, which establishes GAR rates for federated averaging with a
constant
number of local updates. Notably, they apply their rates to an inference procedure based on the multiplier bootstrap approach
[
14
]
and conjecture, without proof, a rate of approximation for this procedure for the Polyak-Ruppert averaged iterates. This remains an important gap in the federated literature, which lacks non-asymptotic methods with formal guarantees for quantifying uncertainty.

In this paper, we aim to close this gap and develop GAR results for federated averaging with an
increasing number of local iterations
, along with an inference procedure based on the multiplier bootstrap with non-asymptotic validity. Our main contributions are as follows:

•

We present new high-order moment bounds for the error of the FedLSA algorithm with decreasing step size and increasing local iterations, capturing the effects of heterogeneity bias and noisy updates. To our knowledge, these are the first moment bounds of order
p
with
p\geq 2
available in the literature.

•

We present the first Gaussian approximation bound for the last iterate of FedLSA (see
Theorem

3
). Our analysis establishes a rate of order up to
t^{-1/2}
in terms of convex distance between probability distributions (see
Section

5
for precise definitions). Our bounds are
fully non-asymptotic
and capture the interplay between the number of local iterations and the choice of step size.

•

We present an inference procedure to approximate the error of the last iterate of FedLSA using the multiplier bootstrap
[
14
]
. Specifically, our bounds show that this distribution can be approximated at a rate of up to
t^{-1/2}
in convex distance, bypassing the Gaussian approximation step involving the asymptotic covariance matrix of the last iterate,
\Sigma_{\infty}
; see
Section

6
. This underscores the distinction between our proposed procedure and various plug-in or batch-mean techniques for constructing confidence intervals, which seek to directly approximate
\Sigma_{\infty}
. Therefore, we provide
the first theoretically grounded method for assessing uncertainty in federated learning
, confirming the hypothesis of
5
.

Notations and definitions.

For a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we denote its operator norm by
\|A\|
. Let
N
be the number of agents; we use the notation
\smash{\mathbb{E}_{c}[a_{c}]=N^{-1}\sum_{c=1}^{N}a_{c}}
for the average over different agents. For a symmetric positive-definite matrix
Q=Q^{\top}\succ 0
,
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the corresponding norm
\|x\|_{Q}=\sqrt{x^{\top}Qx}
, and the respective matrix
Q
-norm of a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
by
\|B\|_{{Q}}=\sup_{x\neq 0}\|Bx\|_{{Q}}/\|x\|_{{Q}}
. For sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we write
a_{n}\lesssim_{\log_{n}}b_{n}
if there exist
c,\alpha>0
(not depending on
n
) such that
a_{n}\leq c(1+\log n)^{\alpha}b_{n}
. In this text, the following abbreviations are used: "w.r.t." stands for "with respect to," "i.i.d." for "independent and identically distributed," and "GAR" for "Gaussian Approximation."

2
Related Work

Federated stochastic approximation.

Most of the federated learning literature has aimed to establish convergence rates for federated averaging under various assumptions
[
35
,
24
,
55
,
32
,
18
,
41
,
51
,
19
]
, along with mechanisms to mitigate the impact of heterogeneity using control variates
[
23
,
36
,
33
,
31
]
, local proximal updates
[
27
,
20
]
, or modified objectives
[
56
,
37
]
.

Finite-sample properties of federated SA were first studied by
12
,
53
,
29
without local training.
25
,
54
subsequently examined federated SA with local updates. More recently,
34
,
61
analyzed federated linear SA with local training and bias correction. In this paper, we extend this line of work by addressing the decreasing-step-size regime alongside an increasing number of local iterations, and by obtaining
p
-moment bounds.

Gaussian approximation for SGD and SA.

Classical asymptotic normality results for stochastic approximation and SGD algorithms date back to foundational papers
[
42
,
26
,
4
]
. Gaussian approximation and Berry-Esseen type bounds for nonlinear statistics of i.i.d. random variables were extensively studied in
49
,
47
. These methods were applied to Polyak–Ruppert averaged linear stochastic approximation in
44
,
58
, and to SGD in
48
, along with non-asymptotic multiplier bootstrap guarantees. The case of stochastic approximation with Markovian noise was recently studied in
59
,
45
,
30
. In the decentralized setting, Gaussian approximation results are much more limited:
5
established Gaussian approximation results for local SGD, including a Berry-Esseen bound for Polyak–Ruppert averaged SA and outlined the corresponding results for the last iterate, both in the regime of a
constant
number of local training steps. In contrast, our results track the heterogeneity-aware error terms that arise in the more general regime of an increasing number of local training steps.

Statistical inference.

Classical methods for constructing confidence sets for optimal parameters are based on direct estimation of the asymptotic covariance matrix using plug-in or batch-mean methods
10
,
7
,
9
. For federated learning,
28
studied asymptotic versions of the functional CLT and their applications to statistical estimation via plug-in and random scaling methods.
21
developed similar inference based on batch-mean methods. Both papers remain asymptotic in their analysis. Alternative approaches use bootstrap and resampling schemes
14
,
60
, with non-asymptotic analysis provided in
44
,
45
. I this paper, we aim to generalize the fully non-asymptotic results obtained in these papers for the setting of Federated LSA.

3
Federated LSA Setting

In this paper, we consider federated linear stochastic approximation (LSA) with
N
agents, who aim to collaboratively solve the following linear system:

\bar{\mathbf{A}}\theta_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}}\,,\penalty\ \text{with}\,\penalty\ \bar{\mathbf{A}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c},\,\bar{\mathbf{b}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c},

(1)

where, for
c\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are distributed among multiple agents.
Furthermore, we assume that the system matrices
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and vectors
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
are unknown, and agent
c
only has access to estimates of
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
.

Stochastic estimation.

To estimate
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
, agent
c
obtains independent samples
Z_{1}^{c},\dots,Z_{T}^{c}
from a distribution
\pi_{c}
, and computes, for
t\in\{1,\dots,T\}
, the estimates
\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t}^{c})
and
\mathbf{b}^{c}(Z_{t}^{c})
such that

\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\pi_{c}}[\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z)]=\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\,,\quad\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\pi_{c}}[\mathbf{b}^{c}(Z)]=\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}\,.

(2)

We introduce the notation for the errors of observations

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}^{c}(z)-\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\,,\quad\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}^{c}(z)=\mathbf{b}^{c}(z)-\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}\,,

(3)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{c}(\theta,z)=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(z)\theta-\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}^{c}(z)\,.

We assume that the
\mathbf{A}^{c}(\cdot)
satisfy the following assumption, which is classical in the LSA literature
[
46
,
34
]
.

A 1

(p)
.

For each agent
c\in[N]
, the matrix
-\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
is Hurwitz, and there exist constants
a>0
and
\eta_{\infty,p}>0
such that

\eta_{\infty,p}a\leq\frac{1}{2}
and for all
0<\eta<\eta_{\infty,p}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mathbb{E}^{1/p}\bigl[\|(\mathrm{I}-\eta\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z))u\|^{p}\bigr]\leq(1-\eta a)\|u\|\,,

for
Z\sim\pi_{c}
with a distribution
\pi_{c}
over
\mathsf{Z}
.

This assumption ensures the existence and uniqueness of
\theta_{\star}
and
\theta^{c}_{\star}
, respectively defined as the solutions of
\bar{\mathbf{A}}\theta_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}}
and
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\theta^{c}_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
.
Given the existence of
\theta^{c}_{\star}
, we will sometimes omit the dependence on
\theta
in
\varepsilon^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star},z)
, denoting

\varepsilon^{c}(z)=\varepsilon^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star},z)=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(z)\theta^{c}_{\star}-\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}^{c}(z)\,.

(4)

To handle stochasticity in the estimates of
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
, we introduce the following quantities:

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon\|_{{\infty}}=\max_{c\in[N]}\sup_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\|\varepsilon^{c}(z)\|\,,\quad\operatorname{C}_{{\bf A}}=\max_{c\in[N]}\sup_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\|\mathbf{A}^{c}(z)\|\,,

which measure observation noise and the stochastic part of the LSA error. Now, we can introduce assumptions about the underlying random process and the stability of the system.

A 2
.

For each agent
c
,
(Z_{k}^{c})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d. random variables with values in
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{Z})
and distribution
\pi_{c}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{c}}[\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{k}^{c})]=\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{c}}[\mathbf{b}(Z_{k}^{c})]=\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
. Moreover, the uniform boundness condition holds

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon\|_{{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\max_{c\in[N]}\sup_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\|\varepsilon^{c}(z)\|<\infty\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{C}_{{\bf A}}

\displaystyle=\max_{c\in[N]}\sup_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\|\mathbf{A}^{c}(z)\|\vee\|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(z)\|<\infty\,.

Heterogeneity.

For federated LSA, a crucial challenge is to ensure that we obtain a solution to the averaged system (
1
), even though
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
differ across agents. To assess the impact of heterogeneity, we measure the differences between
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
using the following two quantities.

\zeta_{1}^{2}\!=\!\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}\!-\theta_{\star})\|^{2}\,,\penalty\ \zeta_{2}^{2}\!=\!\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\!-\bar{\mathbf{A}}\|^{2}\,,

(5)

where,
\zeta_{1}
measures the heterogeneity of the solutions, while
\zeta_{2}
measures the heterogeneity of the matrices
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
. Under the standard assumptions
1
and
2
, these two quantities are always defined and bounded. They play a key role in analyzing the impact of heterogeneity.

Federated LSA algorithm.

To solve the federated LSA problem (
1
), we use the
FedLSA
algorithm
12
,
34
. This method leverages local training on each agent to reduce communication overhead. At each communication round
t
, the server broadcasts the current global model
\theta_{t}
to all agents. Each agent
c
then performs
H_{t}
local stochastic updates using its own data, starting from
\theta_{t,0}^{c}=\theta_{t-1}
and, for
h=1,\dots,H_{t}
, performing the local update

\theta^{c}_{t,h}=\theta^{c}_{t,h-1}-\eta_{t}\big(\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z^{c}_{t,h})\,\theta^{c}_{t,h-1}-\mathbf{b}^{c}(Z^{c}_{t,h})\big)\,,

(6)

where
\eta_{t}
is the learning rate at round
t
. After completing the local updates, the server aggregates the agent models by averaging:
\theta_{t+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\theta^{c}_{t,H_{t}}
. The full procedure is detailed in Algorithm
1
. In contrast to previous work, we consider a decreasing sequence of step sizes along with an increasing sequence of local step numbers.

Algorithm 1

FedLSA

1:

Input:
stepsizes
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
,
initial model
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
number of rounds
T
,
number of agents
N
,
local steps
H_{t}

2:

for

t=1
to
T

do

3:

for

c=1
to
N

do

4:

Initialize current round
\theta^{c}_{t,0}\leftarrow\theta_{t-1}

5:

for

h=1
to
H_{t}

do

6:

Receive data noise
Z^{c}_{t,h}

7:

Compute:
g_{t,h}^{c}\leftarrow\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z^{c}_{t,h})\theta^{c}_{t,h-1}-\mathbf{b}^{c}(Z^{c}_{t,h})

8:

Update local parameter:
\theta_{t,h}^{c}\leftarrow\theta_{t,h-1}^{c}-\eta_{t}g_{t,h}^{c}

9:

Update global parameter
\theta_{t}\leftarrow\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\theta^{c}_{t,H_{t}}

10:

Return:
final parameter
\theta_{T}

4
Moment bounds for FedLSA

We first derive bounds on the moments of the error in FedLSA. First, we bound the second moment of the FedLSA error when using decreasing step sizes and increasing numbers of local iterations. This result will be used in
Section

5
to obtain rates for Gaussian approximation in FedLSA. Next, we extend the analysis to high-order moment bounds that will be used in the analysis of the federated multiplier bootstrap procedure presented in
Section

6
.

4.1
Error Decomposition for FedLSA

To derive our moment bounds, we extend the error decomposition framework from
34
to accommodate non-constant step sizes and numbers of local iterations. We define the following quantities:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{c}_{t,l:r}=\prod\limits_{h=l}^{r}(\mathrm{I}-

\displaystyle\eta_{t,h}\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c}))\,,\,\,\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\Gamma^{c}_{t,1:H_{t}}\,.

For brevity, we will denote
\Gamma^{c}_{t}=\Gamma^{c}_{t,1:H_{t}}
.
We also define the noisy part of these matrices as

\displaystyle\textstyle\Delta_{s,t}=\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-\mathbb{E}\big[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\big]\,,

which captures the fluctuations of the matrix product
\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}
. Using these notations, we obtain the following error decomposition for the last iterate:

\displaystyle\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}=\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(tr)}}+\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(bi,bi)}}+\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(fl,bi)}}+\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(fl)}}\,,

(7)

where we introduce the following notations

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(tr)}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\prod_{s=1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{s}\left\{\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\right\}\,,

(8)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(bi,bi)}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\Big]\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\,,

(9)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(fl,bi)}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}+\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\,,

(10)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(fl)}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\big\{\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\big\}\,.

(11)

The first term,
\smash{\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(tr)}}}
, characterizes the rate at which the initial error decays. The terms
\smash{\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(bi,bi)}}}
and
\smash{\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(fl,bi)}}}
capture the bias and fluctuations arising from statistical heterogeneity. In the special case of homogeneous agents (i.e.,
\smash{\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}=\bar{\mathbf{A}}}
for all agents
c\in[N]
), these two terms vanish. Finally,
\smash{\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl)}}}
accounts for the fluctuations of
\theta_{t}
around the solution
\theta_{\star}
.

4.2
Moment Bounds

We now establish bounds on the moments of the error.
We emphasize that, although similar to
34
’s decomposition, the varying step size and number of local updates change the nature of the terms from decomposition
Equation

7
. Thus we emphasize that the bounds derived in this section do not follow from the ones obtained in
34
. We consider two choices of step size and local update schedules:

S 1

(Constant)
.

For any
t>0
, we set
\eta_{t}=\eta
and
H_{t}=H
for some
0<\eta\leq\eta_{\infty,p}
and
H>0
.

S 2

(Decreasing)
.

For any
t>0
, we set
\eta_{t}=\eta(1+t)^{-\gamma_{\eta}}
and
H_{t}=\lceil H(1+t)^{\gamma_{H}}\rceil
, for some
\eta\in(0,1)
,
H>0
and
\gamma_{\eta}\in[{1\over 2},1)
,
\gamma_{H}\geq 0
, such that
\gamma_{\eta}\geq\gamma_{H}
. Additionally, we assume that
\eta H<a^{-1}
and we denote
\gamma=\gamma_{\eta}-\gamma_{H}
.

The scheme
2
extends the classical polynomially decaying step size regime by allowing the number of local updates to increase over time.
In federated settings, such scaling is natural: as the step size decreases, bias reduces, and performing more local updates per communication round can improve communication efficiency.
The condition
\gamma_{\eta}\geq\gamma_{H}
ensures that the overall contraction remains stable, while
\smash{\eta H<a^{-1}}
guarantees uniform stability of the local dynamics. To our knowledge, this joint regime of decaying step sizes and increasing number of local updates has not been previously analyzed for federated learning.

Second moment bounds.

We first derive a general theorem on the squared error, applicable to any sequence of decreasing step sizes and increasing numbers of local iterations.

Theorem 1
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
. Let
\eta_{s}\leq\eta_{\infty,2}
, be decreasing, and
H_{s}
be increasing, for all
s\geq 0
. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}]\leq\prod_{s=1}^{t}(1-\eta_{s}a)^{H_{s}}\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|

\displaystyle\textstyle+\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}^{2}\exp{\Big(-a\sum_{i=s+1}^{t}\eta_{i}H_{i}\Big)}

\displaystyle\textstyle+\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}+2\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}}{\sqrt{N}}\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}\exp(-2a\sum_{r=1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}))}

\displaystyle\textstyle+\sqrt{\frac{4\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\zeta_{1}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}}{Nae}}\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{5}H_{s}^{4}\exp\big(-a\sum_{r=s+1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}\big)}\,,

where
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}
and
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}
are defined in (
28
).

The proof of
Theorem

1
relies on bounding each term in the error decomposition (
7
). We provide a proof with explicit constants in
Section

A.1
.
When the step size and the number of local updates are constant over time, the above result simplifies and recovers an improved variant of Theorem A.6 in
34
.

Corollary 1
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
and
1
.
Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}]\leq\textstyle(1-\eta a)^{Ht}\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|+\frac{\eta H\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}{a}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\textstyle+\sqrt{\frac{\eta\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{Na}}+\sqrt{\frac{\eta\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{Na}}+\sqrt{\frac{4\eta^{4}H^{3}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\zeta_{1}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}}{Na^{2}e}}\,.

This bound shows that in order to obtain MSE of order
\mathbb{E}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}]\leq\epsilon^{2}
, one needs to choose the step size
\eta\asymp\epsilon^{2}
, and the product
\eta H\asymp\epsilon
.
This choice is due to non-vanishing variance terms.
In contrast, one can make the variance vanish by choosing appropriately decreasing step size.
We thus establish the result on the last iterate moment bounds under the scheme
2
.

Corollary 2
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
and
2
. Then, for all
t\geq 1
, the following bound holds

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\big[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}\big]\lesssim\exp\big(-\frac{a\eta H}{1-\gamma}(t+1)^{1-\gamma}\big)\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|

\displaystyle\textstyle+\frac{8\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta H}{a(t+1)^{\gamma}}+\textstyle\sqrt{\frac{\eta}{Na}}\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}+\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}}{(t+1)^{\gamma_{\eta}/2}}+\sqrt{\tfrac{\eta^{4}H^{3}}{Nae}}\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}{(t+1)^{(4\gamma_{\eta}-3\gamma_{H})/2}}\,.

This corollary shows that, when using decaying step size, one can obtain convergence to arbitrary precision without starting with tiny step sizes.
Specifically, this is the case as long as
\gamma>0
, implying that step sizes must decrease faster than the number of local iteration increases.

Higher-order moment bounds.

Similarly, we establish the result for higher-order moment bounds, which are crucial for further multiplier bootstrap analysis in
Section

6
.

Theorem 2
.

Assume
1
(p),
2
. Let
\eta_{s}\leq\eta_{\infty,2}
, be decreasing, and
H_{s}
be increasing, for all
s\geq 0
. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{E}^{1/p}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{p}]\leq\exp{\left(-a\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}H_{s}\right)}\,\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|

\displaystyle\textstyle+\sqrt{\frac{p^{3}\|\varepsilon\|_{{\infty}}^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}\exp{\left(-2a\sum\limits_{i=s+1}^{t}\eta_{i}H_{i}\right)}}

\displaystyle\textstyle+\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}^{2}\exp{\left(-a\sum_{i=s+1}^{t}\eta_{i}H_{i}\right)}

\displaystyle\textstyle+2\operatorname{C}_{{\bf A}}p^{2}\zeta_{*}\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}\exp{\Big(-2a\sum_{i=s}^{t}\eta_{i}H_{i}\Big)}}

\displaystyle\textstyle+\frac{\operatorname{C}_{{\bf A}}p^{3}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}{\sqrt{Na\mathrm{e}}}\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{5}H_{s}^{4}\exp{\Big(-a\sum_{i=s+1}^{t}\eta_{i}H_{i}\Big)}}\,,

where
\zeta_{*}=1/N\sum_{c=1}^{N}\|\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}\|
.

This theorem is very similar to
Theorem

1
, up to an additional factor depending on
p
.
Following the second-order case, we obtain the counterpart of
Corollaries

1
and
2
, which we state in
Section

A.3
, together with a proof of
Theorem

2
.

5
Gaussian Approximation

5.1
Gaussian approximation framework

In this section, we present our main result, establishing non-asymptotic Gaussian approximation rates for the last iterate of FedLSA. Before presenting particular results, we outline a general scheme for proving the normal approximation in
Section

5.1
.

The classical object studied in the literature on Gaussian approximation
[
8
,
47
]
is the general vector-valued nonlinear statistic
T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which can be represented as

\textstyle T=W+D\,,

(12)

where
W
is a linear statistic of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, and
D
has small moments. We consider the decomposition (
12
) and assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathbb{E}[WW^{\top}]=\mathrm{I}_{d}
. To measure the approximation quality, we focus on the convex distance
\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}
, defined for probability measures
\mu,\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{B\in\mathsf{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|\mu(B)-\nu(B)|\,.

(13)

Estimating
\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}(T,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{I}_{d}))
can be reduced to the following two steps. First, one needs to estimate the convex distance
\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}(W,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{I}_{d}))
using the tools for normal approximation for linear statistics, such as
3
,
47
. In the second step, one proceeds with one of the following approaches:

1.

Estimate moments
\mathbb{E}[\|D\|^{p}]
for some
p\geq 1
and then apply
Proposition

5
(see also
[
48
, Proposition 1]
);

2.

Estimation of the perturbations of the term
D
within the randomized concentration approach
47
, requiring a bound of the fluctuations of the term
D
when one of the variables
X_{i}
, for some
i\in[n]
, is modified.

The first approach is technically simpler, yet it requires one to estimate moments of increasing order
p
, which produces some subtle logarithmic factors in the final convergence rates. The second one is technically more involved, yet it requires only estimating the second moment of the term
D
and provides the bounds that are sharper in terms of extra logarithmic factors.

5.2
Linear statistic of FedLSA

Self-normalized statistic.

To apply Gaussian approximation results for nonlinear statistics of
independent random variables, we extract the leading linear component
of the noise, a linear statistic of
\{Z_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
, and apply
47
. We write the error as follows.

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})=M_{t}+D_{t}^{\mathrm{rm}},

(14)

where
M_{t}
collects the linear statistics of
\{Z_{s,h}^{c}\}
and

D_{t}^{\mathrm{rm}}
is a higher-order remainder term.
Following this decomposition, we define the covariance matrix

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}:=\mathbb{E}[M_{t}M_{t}^{\top}]\,,

(15)

which characterizes the effective variance of the
last iterate. Under
2
,

\Sigma_{t}
converges to the solution of the associated discrete-time Lyapunov equation
\Sigma_{\infty}
, which determines the asymptotic covariance of FedLSA.
We then analyze the self-normalized statistic

\displaystyle T_{t}:=\eta_{t}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}),

(16)

where
\Sigma_{t}
is a covariance matrix representing the main linear part of non-linear statistics
T_{t}
, and is defined in (
15
).
Under the stability
conditions imposed in the previous section,
T_{t}
weakly converges to

\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{I}_{d})
as
t\to\infty
. Our objective is to quantify this
convergence at finite time using the convex distance (
13
).

Linear statistic of FedLSA’s error.

We start from the decomposition

\displaystyle\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}(\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star})

(17)

\displaystyle\quad+\textstyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\big(\Delta^{\sf avg}_{s,H_{s}}-\eta_{s}\mathcal{E}^{\sf avg}_{s,H_{s}}\big),

where we recall that

\displaystyle\textstyle\Delta^{\sf avg}_{t,h}

\displaystyle=\textstyle N^{-1}\sum_{c=1}^{N}(\mathrm{I}-\Gamma_{t,h}^{c})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\sf avg}_{t,h}

\displaystyle=\textstyle N^{-1}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{h}\Gamma_{t,s+1:h}^{c}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{t,s,c}).

The transient term
\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}(\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star})
in (
17
)
is negligible under
1
. The main stochastic contribution arises from the second term, which aggregates agent noise and heterogeneity-induced fluctuations.
By

Corollary

2
, both components contribute at the same
order up to heterogeneity constants (
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
and
\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}
respectively) and must be handled jointly.
To obtain the linear statistic, we center all stochastic contraction matrices around their expectation in (
17
), using the following matrices, defined for
t,l,r\geq 0
,

\displaystyle G_{t,l:r}^{c}=\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{t,l:r}^{c}]\,,\text{ and }G^{\sf avg}_{i}=\mathbb{E}[\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}]\,.

(18)

Unrolling the resulting decomposition and identifying the leading term at each step gives the linear statistic

\displaystyle M_{t}=-N^{-1}\eta_{t}^{-1/2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}G^{\sf avg}_{i}\,(\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{noise}}+\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{het}})\,,

where
\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{noise}}
accounts for the noise directly due to noisy updates, and
\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{het}}
is a noise term which arises due to the heterogeneity of the local solutions,

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{noise}}:=\sum_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=1}^{H_{s}}G_{s,h+1:H_{s}}^{c}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{s,h,c})\,,

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{het}}:=\sum_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=1}^{H_{s}}G_{s,1:h-1}^{c}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(Z_{s,h,c})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\,.

The remainder term is then given by

\displaystyle D_{t}^{\text{rm}}=\eta_{t}^{-1/2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}(\Delta^{\sf avg}_{s,H_{s}}-\eta_{s}\mathcal{E}^{\sf avg}_{s,H_{s}})-M_{t}\,.

Then, we can rewrite our self-normalized statistic
T_{t}
as

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})=W_{t}+D_{t}\,,

(19)

where

W_{t}:=\Sigma_{t}^{-1/2}M_{t}
and
D_{t}=\Sigma_{t}^{-1/2}D_{t}^{\mathrm{rm}}
.

5.3
Last Iterate Gaussian Approximation

We now present the main result of this section. Applying
47
to the decomposition (
19
) yields a non-asymptotic Gaussian approximation for

\smash{\eta_{t}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})}
, leading to the following result.

Theorem 3
.

Under assumptions
1
(2),
2
and
2
, setting
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{I}_{d})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}),Y)

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1/2}(1+t)^{-\gamma_{\eta}/2}+N^{1/2}(1+t)^{-\gamma}

\displaystyle+(1+t)^{-(3\gamma-\gamma_{\eta})/2}\,,

where
\Sigma_{t}
is defined in (
15
).

Theorem

3
extends
[
5
, Theorem 2.2]
- which assume a constant number of local steps (
\gamma_{H}=0
). Note that
Theorem

3
implies, since convex distance is invariant under non-degenerate linear mappings, approximation rates for

\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}),\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t}))\,,

and not for

\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}),\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\infty}))\,,

where
\Sigma_{\infty}
is the limiting covariance matrix of the last iterate of federated LSA defined as the unique solution of the Lyapunov equation (see e.g.
16
):

\bar{\mathbf{A}}\Sigma_{\infty}+\Sigma_{\infty}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}=N^{-1}\Sigma_{*}^{\sf avg}\,.

(20)

Here
\Sigma_{*}^{\sf avg}
is the covariance of noise at
\theta_{\star}

\displaystyle\textstyle\Sigma_{*}^{\sf avg}=N^{-1}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{c}(\theta_{\star},Z_{c})(\varepsilon^{c}(\theta_{\star},Z_{c}))^{\top}]\,.

Although the empirical covariance matrix
\Sigma_{t}
converges to
\Sigma_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
, the rate of convergence may be slow and can therefore dominate the overall Gaussian approximation error. Similar effects were previously observed for Polyak-Ruppert averaged SGD in the single-agent setting in
47
and
48
. Interestingly, the same phenomenon persists for the last iterate bounds. The next proposition quantifies this effect.

Proposition 1
.

Under assumptions
1
(2),
2
, and
2
, and choosing
\eta H\leq\beta_{\infty}
for some
\beta_{\infty}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Sigma_{t}-\Sigma_{\infty}\|\leq C_{\infty,1}(1+t)^{\gamma-1}+C_{\infty,2}(1+t)^{-\gamma}\,,

where the constants
C_{\infty,1}
and
C_{\infty,2}
are given in (
99
) and (
100
).

Proposition

1

reveals an intrinsic trade-off in the choice of the parameter

\gamma=\gamma_{\eta}-\gamma_{H}
.
On the one hand,
\gamma
cannot be too small, as sufficiently fast decay is required to ensure moment control and Gaussian approximation rates.
On the other hand, taking
\gamma
close to
1
deteriorates the covariance stabilization rate through the term

C_{\infty,1}(1+t)^{\gamma-1}\,.

(21)

Interestingly, we can show the lower bound demonstrating that both terms in the bound of
Proposition

1
are essential.

Lower bound
. Consider a
1
-dimensional linear stochastic approximation with single agent (
N=1
):

\bar{\mathbf{A}}\theta_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}},\qquad\bar{\mathbf{A}}=1,\quad\bar{\mathbf{b}}=0\,,

where the unbiased stochastic estimates are given by

\mathbf{A}(Z_{j})=1+\xi_{j},\qquad\mathbf{b}(Z_{j})=\bar{\mathbf{b}}=0,

with
\xi_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Note that in this setting there are no synchronization steps across agents (since
N=1
), so
H_{t}\penalty\ =\penalty\ 1
, and the algorithm reduces to the usual
1
-dimensional LSA. Then, the
FedLSA
algorithm with starting point
\theta_{0}=0
can be written as the recurrence

\theta_{t}=\theta_{t-1}-\eta_{t}(1+\xi_{i})\theta_{t-1},\quad\theta_{0}=0.

(22)

We set the step sizes as
\eta_{t}=1/(1+t)^{\gamma_{\eta}}
, where
\gamma_{\eta}\in(0,1)
. Note that in the single-agent case we have
\gamma_{H}=0
, so that
\gamma=\gamma_{\eta}
. In this case, it is possible to compute the exact value of the limiting variance
\sigma_{\infty}^{2}
and to derive a lower bound for the convex distance:

Proposition 2
.

Assume that
\eta_{1}=0
and
\eta_{\ell}=(1+\ell)^{-\gamma}
with
0<\gamma<1
. For
1
-dimensional single agent
FedLSA
with
\bar{\mathbf{A}}\theta_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{A}}=1,\bar{\mathbf{b}}=0
and
\mathbf{A}(Z_{j})=1+\xi_{j},\mathbf{b}(Z_{j})=\bar{\mathbf{b}}
with
\xi_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
it holds that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\infty}^{2}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{Var}[\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})]=\frac{1}{2}\,.

Moreover, for any
t\geq 4
, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\Big|v_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\Big|\geq\operatorname{C}_{{\bf v}}(t^{\gamma-1}+t^{-\gamma})\,,

(23)

for some positive constant
\operatorname{C}_{{\bf v}}>0
that depends only on
\gamma
.

We provide the proof of this lower bound in Section
B.3
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result that establishes a non-asymptotic lower bound for the last iterate of LSA in terms of the convex distance, thereby extending the lower bound results for Polyak–Ruppert averaging due to
48
.

Discussion.

Theorem

3
demonstrates that increasing the number of local updates over time
(i.e.,
\gamma_{H}>0
) reduces
\gamma
and therefore
deteriorates the rate of normal approximation. While enlarging the number of local iterations may improve
optimization accuracy or communication efficiency, it adversely affects distributional convergence. From the perspective of Gaussian approximation,
this suggests that a constant number of local updates (
\gamma_{H}=0
) is preferable. However, this choice can not always be implementable due to communication constraints.

6
​​​
Multiplier bootstrap for FedLSA

We now aim to perform statistical inference for the last-iterate estimator
\theta_{t}
, and to construct confidence intervals for
\theta_{\star}
. Instead of explicitly estimating the asymptotic covariance matrix
\Sigma_{\infty}
and relying on plug-in Gaussian approximations
[
28
]
, we use an online multiplier bootstrap that directly approximates the sampling distribution of
\theta_{t}
in a data-driven manner.
To this end, we define a bootstrapped sequence, starting with the same initialization as the original FedLSA dynamics, but using randomly weighted local updates.
Bootstrap iterates are defined by

\displaystyle\theta_{t,h}^{\mathsf{b},c}=\theta_{t,h-1}^{\mathsf{b},c}-\eta_{t}w_{t,h,c}\big(\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h,c})\theta_{t,h-1}^{\mathsf{b},c}-\mathbf{b}^{c}(Z_{t,h,c})\big),

where the weights
w_{t,h,c}
are i.i.d. random variables independent of the data
\{Z_{s,h}^{c}\}
which satisfy

\mathbb{E}[w_{1,1,1}]=1,\qquad\mathrm{Var}[w_{1,1,1}]=1.

For ease of notation, we gather these weights in a set

\mathcal{W}^{t}=\{w_{s,h,c}:s\in[t],\,h\in[H_{s}],\,c\in[N]\}\,.

After each block of local updates, the global bootstrap iterate is updated as
\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}=1/N\sum_{c=1}^{N}\theta_{t,H_{t}}^{\mathsf{b},c}
.

Confidence intervals.

Conditionally on the observed data, the distribution of
\smash{\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})}
approximates the sampling distribution of
\smash{\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})}
. More precisely, denoting conditional probability and expectation given the data by

\mathbb{P}^{b}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{Z}^{t}),\qquad\mathbb{E}^{b}(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{Z}^{t})\,.

Our objective, which is the main result of this section, is to
show that the bootstrap validity holds, that is, the quantity

\displaystyle\sup_{B\in\text{Conv}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{b}}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}

\displaystyle(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})\in B)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})\in B)\Big|

(24)

converges to zero as
t\to\infty
, and to establish a rate for this convergence.
This approximation result is essential, as it allows us to construct confidence intervals for the
true solution

\theta_{\star}
based only on the distribution of
\smash{\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})}
. For example, for a coordinate
j\in[d]
and confidence level
(1-\alpha)
, with
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
let
q_{\alpha/2}^{b}
and
q_{1-\alpha/2}^{b}
denote the empirical conditional quantiles of

\smash{\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t,j}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t,j})}
. A
(1-\alpha)
confidence interval for
\theta_{\star}
’s
j
-th coordinate is then

\big[\theta_{t,j}-\eta_{t}^{1/2}q_{1-\alpha/2}^{b},\;\theta_{t,j}-\eta_{t}^{1/2}q_{\alpha/2}^{b}\big].

Similarly, simultaneous confidence intervals can be constructed using bootstrap quantiles of
\smash{\|\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})\|}
.
This approach avoids explicit covariance estimation and remains fully online.
To establish its validity, we first specify the linear statistics of the bootstrap iterates in
Section

6.1
, and then establish our main theorem in
Section

6.2
.

6.1
Bootstrap decomposition

We now aim to extract the linear statistic from
\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t}
.
To this end, we introduce the bootstrap counterparts of matrix product, for
t,\ell,r\geq 0
, as

\displaystyle\textstyle\Gamma_{t,\ell:r}^{\mathsf{b},c}=\prod_{h=l}^{r}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}w_{t,h,c}\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h,c}))\,,\quad\Gamma_{t}^{\mathsf{b},c}=\Gamma_{t,1:H_{t}}^{\mathsf{b},c}\,,

together with
\Gamma^{\mathsf{b},\sf avg}_{t}=N^{-1}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\Gamma_{t}^{\mathsf{b},c}
, the averaged of these bootstrap matrix products.
The main difference between these matrices and the
\smash{\Gamma_{t,\ell:r}}
from
Section

4.1
is that they incorporate the weights
w_{t,h,c}
.
Arguing as in the original process, we obtain the following decomposition of
\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t}
using these bootstrap matrices

\displaystyle\textstyle\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Gamma^{\mathsf{b},\sf avg}_{i}(\theta_{0}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad-\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\mathsf{b},\sf avg}_{i}\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{b},\sf avg}_{s}\,,

where
\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{b},\sf avg}_{s}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{b},\sf avg}_{s}

\displaystyle\textstyle=N^{-1}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=1}^{H_{s}}(w_{s,h,c}-1)\Gamma_{s,h+1:H_{s}}^{\mathsf{b},c}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{c}_{t,h}\,,

with
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{c}_{t,h}=\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{t,h,c})+\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h,c})(\theta_{t,h-1}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star})
.
Note that here, in contrast with the decomposition (
17
), there is no additional heterogeneity term.
This is expected, as we are considering the difference
\smash{\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t}}
and not the error
\smash{\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}}
.
Extracting the leading linear statistics
\smash{M_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}}
in terms of bootstrap weights
w_{s,h,c}
yields the following decomposition

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}})^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})=W_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}+D_{t}^{\mathsf{b}},

(25)

where we define the bootstrap covariance matrix as

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}=\mathbb{E}^{b}\!\left[M_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}(M_{t}^{\mathsf{b}})^{\top}\right]\,,

(26)

together with

D_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}=\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}})^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})-W_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}
, and
W_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}:=(\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}})^{-1/2}M_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}
where the linear term
M_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}
given by

\displaystyle M_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}

\displaystyle\!=\!\textstyle-N^{-1}\eta_{t}^{-1/2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\,\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{noise},\mathsf{b}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{s}^{\mathrm{noise},\mathsf{b}}

\displaystyle\!=\!\textstyle\sum_{c=1}^{N}\!\sum_{h=1}^{H_{s}}(w_{s,h,c}\!-\!1)\Gamma_{s,h+1:H_{s}}^{c}\!\varepsilon^{c}(\theta_{\star},Z_{s,h,c}).

Again, in comparison with results from
Section

5.2
, conditionally on
\mathcal{Z}^{t}
, no heterogeneity term appears, since we compare
\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}
with
\theta_{t}
.

6.2
Bootstrap validity

We now establish the validity of the proposed multiplier bootstrap procedure
for the last-iterate estimator in FedLSA.
To conduct our analysis, we impose one additional technical assumption on the bootstrap weights
\mathcal{W}^{t}
, together with a condition on the minimal number of iterations.

A 3
.

The bootstrap weights
\mathcal{W}^{t}=\{w_{s,h,c}:s\in[t],h\in[H_{s}],c\in[N]\}
satisfy
0<W_{\min}<w_{1,1,1}<W_{\max}<+\infty
almost surely for some
W_{\min},W_{\max}>0
. Additionally, we assume that
\eta<\eta_{\infty,p}/W_{\max}
.

A 4
.

Number of observations
t
is large enough, that is,
t\geq t_{0}
. Precise expression for
t_{0}
is given in Appendix, see
4
.

These assumptions are mostly technical.
Examples of random variables that satisfy the assumption
3
are provided in, e.g.,
48
;

4
ensures sufficient convergence of
\Sigma_{t}
to
\Sigma_{\infty}
and
\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}
to
\Sigma_{t}
, see
Proposition

1
.
We now show that the conditional distribution of the bootstrap statistic provides an accurate Gaussian approximation to the sampling distribution of the normalized estimation error.

Theorem 4

(Bootstrap validity)
.

Assume
1
(
\log(Nt)
),
2
,

2
,

3
,
4
, and let
\eta H\leq\beta_{\infty}
. Then there exists an event
\Omega_{0}
with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{0})\geq 1-4/t
such that,
on this event,

\displaystyle\sup_{B\in\text{Conv}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{b}}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})\in B)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\;-\mathbb{P}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})\in B)\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\log_{t}}\;N^{-1/2}t^{-\gamma_{\eta}/2}+N^{1/2}t^{-\gamma}+t^{-(3\gamma-\gamma_{\eta})/2}\,.

Proof sketch.

The proof relies on Gaussian approximations for both the original and bootstrap iterates, linked via a Gaussian comparison inequality. The main idea of the proof is illustrated with the diagram in
Figure

1
, following the general pipeline outlined in
50
,
44
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Real world:}\;\;\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Bootstrap:}\;\;\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}})\,}
Theorem
3
Gaussian comparison

Proposition

6
Theorem
5

Figure 1
:
Illustration of the multiplier bootstrap bounds: real-world estimates are close to
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t})
, which is itself close to
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}})
.

We define random vectors
Y^{\mathsf{b}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}})
under
\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{b}}
-probability and
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t})
. We define the convex distance under the conditional measure
\mathbb{P}^{b}
as, for random vectors
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}^{\mathsf{b}}(X,Y)=\sup_{B\in\text{Conv}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{b}}(X\in B)-\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{b}}(Y\in B)|\,.

Then the convex distance is split using the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\sup_{B\in\text{Conv}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{b}}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t})\in B)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-\mathbb{P}(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star})\in B)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3},

where we have defined

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}^{\mathsf{b}}\big(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\theta_{t}),Y^{\mathsf{b}}\big),

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{C}}\big(\eta_{t}^{-1/2}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}),Y\big),\quad

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sup_{B\in\text{Conv}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left|\mathbb{P}(Y\in B)-\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{b}}(Y^{\mathsf{b}}\in B)\right|\,.

The term
T_{1}
relates to the Gaussian approximation in the bootstrap world from
Theorem

5
. We estimate it using the decomposition (
25
),
moment bounds for
D_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}
, and the result of

47
. The term
T_{2}
is controlled by the Gaussian approximation result
for the original iterate (
Theorem

3
).
The term
T_{3}
is handled using matrix concentration bounds
for
\|\Sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{b}}-\Sigma_{t}\|
presented in
Proposition

6
.
Combining the bounds implies the result.

Discussion.

The counterpart of
Theorem

4
has been conjectured without the proof for the Polyak-Ruppert averaged federated SGD estimates in
5
. To our knowledge, we provide the first rigorous proof on the accuracy of Gaussian approximation with multiplier bootstrap in the federated setting. Importantly, approximation rates in
Theorem

4
are of order up to
1/\sqrt{t}
(up to logarithmic factors). As already emphasized, this rate is due to the fact that one can bypass approximating the asymptotic covariance matrix
\Sigma_{\infty}
as suggested earlier in
28
. Importantly, normal approximation with
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\infty})
is bypassed even in the proof of
Theorem

4
, and is expected to yield slower approximation rates due to the lower bound (
23
).

0.941_{\scriptscriptstyle\pm 0.007}

Numerical Illustration.

We now numerically illustrate our normal approximation and bootstrap confidence intervals for FedLSA. To this end, we instantiate a Garnet environment
1
,
17
with
n=30
states, feature dimension
d=5
,
a=2
actions, and branching factor
b=2
. We generate
N=5
heterogeneous agents by perturbing this common Garnet MDP, following
34
. We run
R=1024
trajectories and construct confidence intervals for a random one-dimensional projection
u^{\top}\theta_{t}
. For each trajectory, we then check whether
\theta_{\star}
lies within the interval, average this indicator over the
R
trajectories, and report the result as coverage, with the standard error shown as a subscript. For each run of the bootstrap methods, we compute
256
bootstrap trajectories.

Based on our results, we propose two methods (i) empirical bootstrap quantiles (EQ), and (ii) bootstrap standard deviation intervals (SDB). We compare these methods against a plug-in Gaussian approximation baseline, based on an estimated asymptotic covariance (PE). We provide full experimental details, additional confidence levels, and extended tables in Appendix
D
.

We report results for nominal confidence level
\alpha=0.95
using step size
\gamma_{\eta}=0.6
and two different regimes of local updates: in Table
1
with a constant number of local updates (
\gamma_{H}=0
), and in Table

2
with an increasing number of local updates (
\gamma_{H}=0.2
).
In both cases, our methods (SDB and EQ) achieve better coverage during early iterations (
T=2000
and
T=6000
), highlighting the superiority of these methods in regimes that are far from the asymptotic behavior.
In contrast, as the number of total samples grow, all methods reach comparable coverage. This indicates that the plug-in covariance estimate improves as more samples are collected, and the algorithm reaches its asymptotic behavior.
Finally, we note that using a constant number of local step sizes may allow for identifying confidence intervals with low precision earlier, while using an increasing number of local step sizes allows to reach good coverage with fewer communications.

Table 2

Coverage for nominal confidence level
\alpha=0.95
with
\gamma_{H}=0.2
. Standard deviations are shown as subscripts.

8
Conclusion

We derived non-asymptotic Berry-Esseen–type bounds for the last iterate of FedLSA, providing finite-sample Gaussian approximation guarantees under heterogeneous data and communication constraints. Our results quantify the joint impact of the number of agents, step-size decay, and the schedule of local updates on the normal approximation rate.
To derive these results, we established a new analysis of the convergence of FedLSA with decreasing step size and increasing number of local iterations, providing the first bounds on higher-order moments for this method. Based on this result, we established the validity of an online multiplier bootstrap procedure for FedLSA, which approximates the distribution of the normalized last iterate without requiring estimation of the asymptotic covariance matrix.
Our results enable practical construction of confidence intervals and uncertainty quantification in federated settings.

Appendix

Table of Contents

Appendix A
Proof for moment bounds

We are trying to solve the following linear equation system across
N
clients
c=1\ldots N
:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{A}}\theta_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}}

where
\bar{\mathbf{A}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
.

Let
\theta_{\star}
be the unique solution of
\bar{\mathbf{A}}\theta_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}}
and
\theta^{c}_{\star}
the unique solution of
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\theta^{c}_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
. Both
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}
and
\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
are not observed directly in our setting. Instead, we could observe
A^{c}(Z_{t}^{c})
and
b^{c}(Z_{t}^{c})
during
t
-th iteration at
c
-th client. For
z\in\mathsf{Z}
we denote the stochastic part of the matrices and vectors
A^{c}(z)
and
b^{c}(z)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(z)=\mathbf{A}^{c}(z)-\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\,,\quad\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}^{c}(z)=\mathbf{b}^{c}(z)-\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}\,,

(27)

as well as the noise representations

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{c}(z)=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(z)\theta^{c}_{\star}-\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}^{c}(z)\,,\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\omega^{c}(z)=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(z)\theta_{\star}-\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}^{c}(z)\,.

The first corresponds to the intrinsic stochastic noise around the local solution,
while the second measures the stochastic perturbation evaluated at the global solution. To control stochastic fluctuations, we define uniform bounds

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon\|_{{\infty}}=\max_{c\in[N]}\sup_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\|\varepsilon^{c}(z)\|\,,\quad\operatorname{C}_{{\bf A}}=\max_{c\in[N]}\sup_{z\in\mathsf{Z}}\|\mathbf{A}^{c}(z)\|\,.

and the two following quantities, which measure variance

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{c}\!\left[\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\right\|\,\|\theta_{\star}^{c}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}\right]\,,\qquad\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{c}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{c}\right)\right]\,,\qquad\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\|^{2}]

(28)

Finally, we define the two following quantities, that define first and second order heterogeneity

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\|^{2}\,,\qquad\zeta_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}-\bar{\mathbf{A}}\|^{2}\,.

(29)

A.1
Expansion of the error of FedLSA

We first express a single local update of
FedLSA
in terms of the local error

\theta_{t,h}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star}
. Using
\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\theta^{c}_{\star}=\bar{\mathbf{b}}^{c}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\theta_{t,h}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t,h}\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c}))(\theta_{t,h-1}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star})-\eta_{t,h}\left(\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})\theta^{c}_{\star}-\mathbf{b}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t,h}\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c}))(\theta_{t,h-1}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star})-\eta_{t,h}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})\,.

(30)

Iterating the recursion over
h=1,\dots,H_{t}
yields

\displaystyle\theta_{t,H_{t}}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star}

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{c}_{t,1:H_{t}}(\theta_{t,0}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star})-\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{t}}\eta_{t,h}\Gamma^{c}_{t,h+1:H_{t}}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})\,,

(31)

where we defined the following matrix products

\displaystyle\Gamma^{c}_{t,l:r}=\prod\limits_{h=l}^{r}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t,h}\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c}))\,,\qquad\Gamma^{c}_{t}=\Gamma^{c}_{t,1:H_{t}}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\Gamma^{c}_{t}\,.

(32)

We now derive the global recursion for the error

\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}
. Since

\theta_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\theta_{t,H_{t}}^{c}
,
we write

\displaystyle\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\theta_{t,H_{t}}^{c}-\theta_{\star}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}(\theta_{t,H_{t}}^{c}-\theta^{c}_{\star})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}).

Substituting (
31
) and using

\theta_{t,0}^{c}=\theta_{t-1}
gives

\displaystyle\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\Gamma_{t}^{c}(\theta_{t-1}-\theta^{c}_{\star})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=1}^{H_{t}}\eta_{t,h}\Gamma^{c}_{t,h+1:H_{t}}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}).

Rearranging terms, we obtain the compact global error recursion

\displaystyle\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{t}(\theta_{t-1}-\theta_{\star})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}(\mathrm{I}-\Gamma_{t}^{c})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=1}^{H_{t}}\eta_{t,h}\Gamma^{c}_{t,h+1:H_{t}}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c}).

We may decompose
\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}
as

\displaystyle\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}=\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{t}(\theta_{t-1}-\theta_{\star})+\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}+\tau^{\sf avg}_{t}+\varphi^{\sf avg}_{t}\,,

(33)

where we introduced following notations for
FedLSA
’s deterministic bias
\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}
and its fluctuations
\tau^{\sf avg}_{t}
, as well as the fluctuations of the updates
\varphi^{\sf avg}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}(\mathrm{I}-G_{t}^{c})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\,,\quad\tau^{\sf avg}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}(G_{t}^{c}-\Gamma^{c}_{t,1:H_{t}})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\,,

(34)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\sf avg}_{t}=-\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{t}}\eta_{t,h}\Gamma_{t,h+1:H_{t}}^{c}\varepsilon^{c}(Z^{c}_{t,h})\,,

(35)

as well as the following matrix notations, representing the expected value of the
\Gamma_{t,l:r}^{c}
,
\Gamma_{t}^{c}
, and
\Gamma_{t}^{\textrm{avg}}
matrices

\displaystyle G_{t,l:r}^{c}=\prod_{h=l}^{r}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t,h}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})\,,\qquad G_{t}^{c}=G_{t,1:H_{t}}\,,\qquad G^{\sf avg}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}G_{t}^{c}\,.

(36)

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{t,l:r}^{c}]=G_{t,l:r}^{c},\qquad\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{t}^{c}]=G_{t}^{c}\,.

Unrolling (
33
), we obtain the following decomposition of the global iterate’s error at step
t
,

\displaystyle\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}

\displaystyle=\prod_{s=1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{s}(\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star})+\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\prod_{j=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{j}(\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}+\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}+\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(tr)}}+\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(bi, bi)}}+\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl, bi)}}+\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl)}}\,.

(37)

Where we introduced the notations

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{tr})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{s=1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{s}\left\{\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\right\}

(38)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(bi, bi)}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\Big]\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}

(39)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl, bi)}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}+\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}

(40)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl)}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\Big\{\prod\limits_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\}\,,

(41)

where we also defined
\Delta_{s,t}=\left\{\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\right\}-\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\Big]
, the fluctuations of the matrix product
\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}
.

Finally, we define the past and future filtrations

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{s,h}^{-}

\displaystyle=\sigma\!\left(Z_{t,k}^{c}:t<s\text{ or }(t=s,\ k\leq h)\right),

(42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{s,h}^{+}

\displaystyle=\sigma\!\left(Z_{t,k}^{c}:t>s\text{ or }(t=s,\ k\geq h)\right).

(43)

Here,
\mathcal{F}_{s,h}^{-}
represents the noise up to iteration
(s,h)
,
while
\mathcal{F}_{s,h}^{+}
represents the future noise starting from
(s,h)
.

A.2
Proofs for the second moment bound
\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}]

Transient term.

First, we give a bound on the transient term
\tilde{\theta}^{\sf{(tr)}}_{t}
, which quantifies the impact of the initialization.

Lemma 1
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
, and let
\eta_{s}\leq\eta_{\infty,2}
. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\tilde{\theta}^{\sf{(tr)}}_{t}\|^{2}]\leq\prod_{s=1}^{t}(1-\eta_{s}a)^{H_{s}}\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|\,.

Proof.

Expanding
\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(tr)}}
using (
8
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(tr)}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\Big[\Big\|\prod_{s=1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{s}\cdot(\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star})\Big\|^{2}\Big]=\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\Big[\Big\|\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\Gamma_{t,1:H_{t}}^{c}\prod_{s=1}^{t-1}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{s}\cdot(\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star})\Big\|^{2}\Big]\,.

Using (
1
) recursively then allows to bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\mathrm{(tr)}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\Big[\Big\|\Gamma_{t,1:H_{t}}\prod_{s=1}^{t-1}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{s}\cdot(\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star})\Big\|^{2}\Big]\leq\prod_{s=1}^{t}(1-\eta_{s}a)^{H_{s}}\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|\,,

which is the result of the lemma.
∎

Bound on heterogeneity.

To assess the impact of heterogeneity, we first give a bound on the accumulated heterogeneity between two communications.

Lemma 2
.

Assume
2
,
1
, and let
\eta_{s}\leq\eta_{\infty}
for all
s\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle\|\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}\|\leq\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}H_{t}^{2}}{2}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\,.

Proof.

We start by recalling the definition (
34
) of
\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}
, and expanding the difference of matrices using
Lemma

22

\displaystyle\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}(\mathrm{I}-G_{t}^{c})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=0}^{H_{t}-1}\eta_{t}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{h}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\,.

where we used the fact that
G_{t}^{c}=(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{H_{t}}
is defined in (
36
).
Moreover, since
\sum_{c=1}^{N}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})=0
, we can further write

\displaystyle\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=0}^{H_{t}-1}\eta_{t}\Big((\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{h}-(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}})^{h}\Big)\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum_{h=0}^{H_{t}-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{h-1}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{\ell-1}(\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}-\bar{\mathbf{A}})(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}})^{h-\ell-1}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\,.

Taking the norm of this equality, using the triangle inequality, bounding matrices using
\|\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\|\leq 1
, and computing the sum, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\rho^{\sf avg}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}H_{t}(H_{t}-1)}{2N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}-\bar{\mathbf{A}}\|\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}H_{t}(H_{t}-1)}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}-\bar{\mathbf{A}}\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N}\|\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\,,

where we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the second inequality.
The result follows from the definition of heterogeneity (
29
)
∎

And we can track the propagation of this bias through successive communications.

Bound on the bias.

Next, we provide the bound on
\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(bi,bi)}}
second moment bounds.

Lemma 3
.

Under assumptions
1
(2),
2
and
2
it holds that:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(bi,bi)}}\|\leq\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}^{2}\exp{\left(-a\sum_{i=s+1}^{t}\eta_{i}H_{i}\right)}\,.

Proof.

By the definition of
\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(bi, bi)}}
in (
9
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(bi, bi)}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\Big\|\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\Big]\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\|=\Big\|\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\,\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big]\Big\|\,.

By applying
1
recursively along with
Lemma

2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(bi, bi)}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}(1-\eta_{i}a)^{H_{i}}\,\frac{\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}^{2}}{2}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\leq\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}^{2}\exp{\left(-a\sum_{i=s+1}^{t}\eta_{i}H_{i}\right)}\,,

which is the result of the lemma.
∎

Bound on fluctuations.

We then bound the fluctuations of the algorithm, which decompose in a term
\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(fl)}}
, corresponding to the fluctuations of the updates, and a term
\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl, bi)}}
which quantifies the fluctuations of the bias.
We start with
\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(fl)}}
, showing that the algorithm’s variance scales in
1/N
.

Lemma 4
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
and let
\eta_{s}\leq\eta_{\infty,2}
for all
s\geq 0
. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(fl)}}\|^{2}]\leq\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\exp(-2a{S}_{s+1:t})\cdot(H_{s}\eta_{s}^{2}\wedge\frac{\eta_{s}}{a})\cdot\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{N}\,.

Proof.

Defining
X_{s}=\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}
for
s\geq 0
, we can expand (
11
) as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl)}}\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\prod\limits_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\|^{2}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}X_{s}\Big\|^{2}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum\limits_{s,r=1}^{t}X_{s}^{\top}X_{r}\Big]\,.

(44)

Assuming
r>s
we get the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[X_{s}^{\top}X_{r}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[X_{s}^{\top}X_{r}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s+1,1}^{+}\Big]\Big]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\Big[\left(\varphi^{\sf avg}_{r}\right)^{\top}\cdot\prod_{i=r+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\times\prod_{j=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{j}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s+1,1}^{+}\Big]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\varphi^{\sf avg}_{r}\right)^{\top}\cdot\prod_{i=r+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\times\prod_{j=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{j}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Big[\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{s+1,1}^{+}\Big]}_{\text{0}}\right]=0\,.

Plugging this in (
44
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl)}}\|^{2}]=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\|^{2}\Big]\,.

We now bound each term of this sum using
1
conditionally, which gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\Big[\Big\|\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\Big[\Big\|\Gamma_{t,1:H_{t}}^{c}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t-1}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\eta_{t}a)^{H_{t}}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\Big[\Big\|\prod_{i=s+1}^{t-1}\bar{\Gamma}_{i}\cdot\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=s+1}^{t}(1-\eta_{j}a)^{H_{j}}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]\,.

(45)

Finally, we bound
\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]
. As
\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{s,h+1:H_{s}}^{c}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{s,h}^{c})|\mathcal{F}_{s,h+1}^{+}]=0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{s}}\mathbb{E}[\|\eta_{s}\Gamma_{s,h+1:H_{s}}^{c}\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{s,h}^{c})\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{s}}\eta_{s}^{2}(1-\eta_{s}a)^{2(H_{s}-h)}\mathbb{E}[\|\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{s,h}^{c})\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{s}^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\|\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{s,h}^{c})\|^{2}]\cdot\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{s}}(1-\eta_{s})^{2(H_{s}-h)}\,.

Since
\sum\limits_{h=0}^{H_{s}-1}(1-\eta_{s}a)^{2h}\leq H_{s}\wedge\frac{1}{\eta_{s}a}
and
\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[\|\varepsilon^{c}(Z_{s,h}^{c})\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{c}[\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{c})]=\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\varphi^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]\leq\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{N}\cdot(H_{s}\eta_{s}^{2}\wedge\frac{\eta_{s}}{a})\,.

(46)

Plugging (
46
) in (
45
) and using
\prod_{j=s+1}^{t}(1-\eta_{j}a)^{H_{j}}\leq\exp(-a{S}_{s+1:t})
gives the result.
∎

Fluctuations of the bias

. We now bound
\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl, bi)}}
, the fluctuations of the bias.
We show that it also scales in
1/N
.

Lemma 5
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
and let
\eta_{s}\leq\eta_{\infty,2}
, for all
s\geq 0
, be a decreasing sequence. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(fl,bi)}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{4\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{N}}\sqrt{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{2}H_{s}\exp(-2a\sum_{r=s}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r})}+\sqrt{\frac{4\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\zeta_{1}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}}{Nae}}\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{5}H_{s}^{4}\exp\big(-a\sum_{r=s+1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}\big)}\,.

Proof.

Starting from (
10
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{(fl, bi)}}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{T_{1}}}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\left\{\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma_{i}^{\textrm{avg}}\Big]\right\}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{T_{2}}}}\,.

(47)

Bound on
\bm{\mathrm{T_{1}}}
.

We start by deriving a bound on
\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}(G_{s}^{c}-\Gamma_{s}^{c})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\|(G_{s}^{c}-\Gamma_{s}^{c})(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\|^{2}]}_{\bm{\mathrm{U_{c}}}}\,.

(48)

Using
Lemma

22
to rewrite the matrix product difference, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{U_{c}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\Big(\prod_{h=1}^{H_{t}}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})-\prod_{h=1}^{H_{t}}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c}))\Big)(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\eta_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{t}}(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{h-1}(\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c}-\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c}))\Gamma_{t,h+1:H_{t}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\Big\|^{2}\Big]\,.

Noticing that
\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{h-1}(\mathbf{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})-\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})\Gamma_{t,h+1:H_{t}}^{c}|\mathcal{F}_{t,h+1}^{+}]=0
, we write

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{U_{c}}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{t}^{2}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{t}}\mathbb{E}[\|(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{t}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{h-1}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})\Gamma_{t,h+1:H_{t}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{t}^{2}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{t}}(1-\eta_{t}a)^{2(h-1)}\mathbb{E}\Big[(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})^{\top}\Gamma_{t,h+1:H_{t}}^{c\top}\tilde{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})^{\top}\tilde{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})\Gamma_{t,h+1:H_{t}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})\Big]\,.

As
v:=\Gamma_{t,h+1:H_{t}}^{c}(\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star})
is
\mathcal{F}_{t,h+1}^{+}
-measurable, the law of total expectation gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[v^{\top}\tilde{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})^{\top}\tilde{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})v]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[v^{\top}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\tilde{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})^{\top}\tilde{A}^{c}(Z_{t,h}^{c})|\mathcal{F}_{t,h+1}^{+}]\cdot v\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[v^{\top}\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}v\Big]\,.

Then we can estimate
\mathbb{E}[v^{\top}\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}v]
by using the fact that
\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}
is symmetric

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[v^{\top}\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}v]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\|(\Sigma_{\tilde{A}}^{c})^{1/2}v\|^{2}]\leq\|\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}\|\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|v\|^{2}]\leq(1-\eta_{t}a)^{2(H_{t}-h)}\|\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}\|\cdot\|\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}\,.

Hence, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle\!\!\bm{\mathrm{U_{c}}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{t}^{2}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{t}}(1-\eta_{t}a)^{2(H_{t}-1)}\|\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}\|\|\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}=H_{t}\eta_{t}^{2}(1-\eta_{t}a)^{2(H_{t}-1)}\|\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}\|\|\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}\,.

(49)

Plugging (
49
) in (
48
), we obtain the following bound on
\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{H_{s}\eta_{s}^{2}(1\!-\!\eta_{s}a)^{2(H_{s}\!-1)}}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\|\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}\|\|\theta^{c}_{\star}\!-\!\theta_{\star}\|^{2}\!=\!H_{s}\eta_{s}^{2}(1\!-\!\eta_{s}a)^{2(H_{s}\!-1)}\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{N}\,.

(50)

Here, we recall that
\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\|\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}}\|\|\theta^{c}_{\star}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}
.
Now, using the definition of
{S}_{s+1:t}
, we have the following bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\mathrm{T_{1}}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\|\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\cdot\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]\leq\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\exp(-2a{S}_{s+1:t})\mathbb{E}[\|\tau^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]\,.

Using (
50
) and
\eta_{s}a\leq 1/2
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\mathrm{T_{1}}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\exp(-2a{S}_{s+1:t})(1\!-\!\eta_{s}a)^{2(H_{s}\!-1)}H_{s}\eta_{s}^{2}\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\exp(-2a{S}_{s:t})H_{s}\eta_{s}^{2}\frac{\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{N}\,.

(51)

Bound on
\bm{\mathrm{T_{2}}}
.

Now we need to bound
\bm{\mathrm{T_{2}}}=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}
, where we recall that
\Delta_{s,t}=\{\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}\}-\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}]
.
Let’s begin with a single summand
\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}
, which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-\prod_{i=s+1}^{t}G_{i}^{\textrm{avg}}\Big)\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum\limits_{i=s+1}^{t}\Big\{\!\!\prod_{r=i+1}^{t}\!\!\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{r}\Big\}\cdot\Big\{\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-G^{\textrm{avg}}_{i}\Big\}\cdot\Big\{\!\!\prod_{r=s+1}^{i-1}\!\!G^{\textrm{avg}}_{r}\Big\}\Big)\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\,.

We first bound the norm of
(\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-G^{\textrm{avg}}_{i})u
for an arbitrary vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i\geq 0
.
By clients’ independence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|(\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-G^{\textrm{avg}}_{i})u\|^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}(\Gamma_{i,1:H_{i}}^{c}-G_{i,1:H_{i}}^{c})u\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\|(\Gamma_{i,1:H_{i}}^{c}-G_{i,1:H_{i}}^{c})u\|^{2}\big]\,.

Then,
Lemma

22
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|(\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-G^{\textrm{avg}}_{i})u\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{i}^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{i}}\mathbb{E}[\|(\mathrm{I}-\eta_{i}\bar{\mathbf{A}}^{c})^{h-1}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{c}(Z_{i,h}^{c})\Gamma_{i,h+1:H_{i}}^{c}u\|^{2}]\,.

By applying conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{i+1,1}^{+}]
, we can rewrite it as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|(\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-G^{\textrm{avg}}_{i})u\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{i}^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{c=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{h=1}^{H_{i}}(1-\eta_{i}a)^{2(h-1)}\mathbb{E}[\|(\Sigma^{c}_{\mathbf{A}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma_{i,h+1:H_{i}}^{c}u\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\eta_{i}a)^{2(H_{i}-1)}\frac{\eta_{i}^{2}H_{i}}{N}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\|u\|^{2}\,.

Using this knowledge, we can finally estimate
\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}
’s squared norm

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big\|\sum\limits_{i=s+1}^{t}\{\prod_{r=i+1}^{t}\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{r}\}\cdot(\Gamma^{\sf avg}_{i}-G^{\textrm{avg}}_{i})\cdot\{\prod_{r=s+1}^{i-1}G^{\textrm{avg}}_{r}\}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\Big\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{i=s+1}^{t}\frac{\eta_{i}^{2}H_{i}}{N}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\prod_{r=i+1}^{t}(1-\eta_{r}a)^{2H_{r}}(1-\eta_{i}a)^{2H_{i}-2}\prod_{r=s+1}^{i-1}(1-\eta_{r}a)^{2H_{r}}\|\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\Big\{\prod_{r=s+1}^{t}(1-\eta_{r}a)^{2H_{r}}\Big\}\sum\limits_{i=s+1}^{t}\frac{\eta_{i}^{2}H_{i}}{N}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\|\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}\,.

where we used
1-\eta_{i}a\geq 1/2
.
Using the fact that the step size decreases, and the fact that for all
c>0
and
x\geq 0
,
x\exp(-2cx)\leq 1/(ec)\exp(-cx)
, we obtain the following bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\Delta_{s,t}\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\eta_{s}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}}{N}\|\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}\exp\big(-2a\sum_{r=s+1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}\big)\sum\limits_{r=s+1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\eta_{s}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}}{Nae}\|\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}\exp\big(-a\sum_{r=s+1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}\big)\,.

(52)

Using the reverse martingale structure, (
52
), and
Lemma

2
, we obtain a bound on
\mathbb{E}[\|T_{2}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\mathrm{T_{2}}}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}}{Nae}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}\|\rho^{\sf avg}_{s}\|^{2}\exp\big(-a\sum_{r=s+1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}}{Nae}\zeta_{1}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}^{5}H_{s}^{4}\exp\big(-a\sum_{r=s+1}^{t}\eta_{r}H_{r}\big)\,.

(53)

The result follows from taking the root of the expected squared norm of (
47
), decomposing it two using Minkowski’s inequality, and bounding each term using (
51
) and (
53
).
∎

Convergence results.

Combining our results, we can now give an upper bound on the error and prove
Theorem

1
.

A.2.1
Proof of the
Theorem

1

Proof.

Using the decomposition (
37
) and applying Minkowski’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\big[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\big[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{tr})}\|^{2}\big]+\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\big[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{bi,bi})}\|^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\big[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{fl,bi})}\|^{2}\big]+\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\big[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{fl})}\|^{2}\big]

The four terms are controlled respectively by

Lemma

1
,

Lemma

3
,

Lemma

5
,
and
Lemma

4
.
Combining these bounds yields the claimed result.
∎

When the step size and number of local updates are constant, we recover a slightly improved variant of the Theorem A.6 of
34
.

Corollary 3

(Constant step size)
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
and
1
. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\eta a)^{Ht}\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|+\frac{\eta H\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}{a}+\sqrt{\frac{\eta\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{Na}}+\sqrt{\frac{\eta\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{Na}}+\sqrt{\frac{4\eta^{4}H^{3}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\zeta_{1}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}}{Na^{2}e}}\,,

Proof.

By
Theorem

1
with fixed step size and number of local updates, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\eta a)^{Ht}\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|+\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta^{2}H^{2}\exp{\Big(-a\eta H(t-s)\Big)}+\sqrt{\frac{\eta^{2}H\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{N}}\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\exp(-2a\eta Ht)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{\frac{4\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{N}}\sqrt{\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\eta^{2}H\exp(-2a\eta^{2}H(t-s))}+\sqrt{\frac{4\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\zeta_{1}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}}{Nae}}\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\eta^{5}H^{4}\exp\big(-a\eta H(t-s)\big)}\,.

Bounding the sums, for
u>0
, of
\sum_{s=1}^{t}\exp(-u(t-s))\leq(1-\exp(-u))^{-1}\leq 1/u
gives the result.
∎

Finally, with decreasing step sizes and number of local updates, we obtain

Corollary 4
.

Assume
1
(2),
2
and
2
, meaning that the step sizes and local updates satisfy,
\eta_{t}=\frac{\eta}{(t+1)^{\gamma_{\eta}}}
,
H_{t}=\lceil{H}{(t+1)^{\gamma_{H}}}\rceil
,
for some
\eta,H>0
and
0<\gamma_{\eta}<1
and
0<\gamma_{H}<1
, such that
\gamma_{\eta}\geq\gamma_{H}
and
\gamma=\gamma_{\eta}-\gamma_{H}\neq 1
.
Then, for all
t\geq 1
, the following bound holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/2}\big[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{a\eta H}{1-\gamma}(t+1)^{1-\gamma}\Big)\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|+\frac{8\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta H}{a}\frac{1}{(t+1)^{\gamma}}+\sqrt{\frac{8\eta\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{Na}}\frac{1}{(t+1)^{\gamma_{\eta}/2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{\frac{32\eta\bar{\sigma}_{\textrm{het}}^{2}}{Na}}\frac{1}{(t+1)^{\gamma_{\eta}/2}}+\sqrt{\frac{32\eta^{4}H^{3}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbf{A}}^{2}\zeta_{1}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}}{Nae}}\frac{1}{(t+1)^{(4\gamma_{\eta}-3\gamma_{H})/2}}\,.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

25
to the result of
Theorem

1
, thus obtain the bound.
∎

A.3
Proofs for high-order moment bounds
\mathbb{E}^{1/p}[\|\theta_{t}-\theta_{\star}\|^{p}]
.

To prove
Theorem

2
,
we establish moment bounds for each component in the decomposition

\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{tr})}
,

\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{fl})}
,

\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{fl,bi})}
,
and

\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{(\mathrm{bi,bi})}
.

Bound on the transient term.

We begin with a bound on the
p
-th moment of the transient component.

Lemma 6
.

Assume
1
(p),
2
, and let
\eta_{s}\leq\eta_{\infty,p}
. Then, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1/p}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(tr)}}\|^{p}]\leq\exp{\Big(-a\sum\limits_{s=1}^{t}\eta_{s}H_{s}\Big)}\,\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{\star}\|\,.

Proof.

We start with bounding the transient term
\mathbb{E}^{1/p}[\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}^{\sf{(tr)}}\|^{p}]
. It holds that
